| A Python record that lost its groups was not said, and its target read `not_loaded` | corrected | One macOS CI run of `frozen_garbage_is_tied_to_the_freeze_site_by_a_count_column_and_reads_growth` (a flake: the same test passed on the same image just before) got a series with `py.traced` rising as designed (904 bytes per unit) and no `py.gc_frozen` group; `dla trend` read the target `not_loaded` ("the workload never loaded that code") and nothing in `dla harness run` or the series said a record was reduced. The cause on that machine is not established (no macOS here). Forcing the path on Linux (the agent copy of the harness cannot read the freeze site: `gc.freeze` is still called and the dump has no group) gave the same series: columns without `py.gc_frozen`, `other_targets {not_loaded: 1}`, `py.traced` slope 904. Now: the agent keeps the group under `<non-project>` when the site lookup fails and writes `reduced record: ...` when the group cannot be written; the typed record reader remembers the sections it had to drop (`probes`, `types`, ..., `groups`) and a Python record that lacks `types`, `probes` or `limitations` counts as cut short; the series gets `reduced record: the record of point N lost ...`, the run output a `REDUCED RECORD` `_attention` line, and `dla trend` reads a target whose probe has no column as `unknown` with the command that measures again. The test explains itself: its panic message carries `_attention`, the series' limitations and column names. Tests: `a_reduced_record_is_said_and_its_target_reads_unknown_not_not_loaded` (`reduced_record.rs`; fails without the verdict rule, without the `_attention` line and without the series limitation) | The macOS cause is open: the typed reader's drop order (probes, types, coverage, detectors, limitations, purge, groups) and the swallowed freeze-site failure both fit the symptom, and neither was shown on macOS; a record cut short in a way that keeps `types`, `probes` and `limitations` and loses only a group is said only when the agent says it |
| A JVM project with tests was asked for hundreds of hand-written units | corrected | `--junit --next` is the first pass of the queue: it takes the next ranked candidates that have a test class (16 targets, 10 classes, 1000 tests at most), chooses the classes by name then by mention and prints them; the queue, status, gate, trend, close-rest and the report line say "N candidates in M test-loop batches, nothing to write; then K candidates need a driver"; a flat test-loop reading is one reading (second look), a miss gets a focus harness; a failed batch prints the driver command; the loop drops a class that cannot run and fits the time limit; trend names the column of a declared target first; the report line no longer counts a candidate a tied growth settled as waiting for the test loop. Tests: `crates/dla-core/src/testloop.rs` unit tests, `queue::tests::*test_loop*`, `a_candidate_settled_by_a_tied_growth_*`, `harness_junit` `next_with_junit_*`, `more_than_eight_targets_*`, `a_class_that_cannot_run_is_dropped_*`, `the_loop_fits_its_runs_*`, `a_test_loop_that_cannot_build_or_measure_*`, `harness_run_attempt` `a_test_loop_that_does_not_build_*`, `the_column_of_a_declared_target_leads_the_passed_columns`; each seen failing with a marker mutation (by name, by mention, the target bound of the queue and of gen, `--next` taking the batch, both driver-fallback texts, the drop of a class with no passing test, the drop of the slowest class, the declared-first order, the report filter), then reversed. Real tree (a JVM library with 309 JUnit 5 test classes, Maven, 597 findings, 388 ranked): the printed commands (`gen --junit --next`, `run`, `trend`) ran 24 test-loop batches of 2 to 16 targets and 15 to 194 tests, 34 to 82 s each (mean 52 s, about 25 min in all of the 3 h budget), and read 4 `grew`, 213 `executed`, 127 missed once; the finding at `TypedPropertyPaths.java:53` was in batch 2 and the printed route (sibling, `ledger test`, `alloc-test`, refute, confirm) ended `confirmed`. A review of that change then corrected: `dla census --lang jvm` writes the workload discovery result when the run has none (an earlier real run never ran `dla workload discover`, so the loop would not have been offered to it); the classes of one pick are of one module and fit one harness; a flat reading of a target whose chosen classes were all dropped reads `not_executed`; a run of the tests in which some fail and others pass is kept (before, one test that failed in every run failed the harness); the time of a run is measured again after a class is dropped; a bare `--next` with `--junit --tests` takes 16; the candidate of a generated test-loop harness is told to run it; `dla harness run` names a dropped class and a lowered step first; the sentences that name the next step are never cut. Tests: `the_classes_of_one_pick_*`, `a_flat_reading_of_a_test_loop_target_whose_test_class_was_dropped_is_a_miss`, `a_jvm_census_discovers_the_tests_*`, `a_test_that_fails_in_every_run_*`, `next_with_junit_and_a_probe_*`, `a_generated_test_loop_harness_is_run_*`, `a_missed_candidate_is_told_*`, the attention-lines test, `the_sentences_that_name_the_next_step_*`, `the_last_resort_keeps_the_sentences_*`; each seen failing with a marker mutation, then reversed |  Candidates without a test (41 at the start, 132 pending at the end with the missed ones) still need hand-written drivers, not run; `dla-bench` not run (it opens its runs with the queue off, so the test loop does not change it); a flat test-loop reading gets a second look for 9 candidates at most, the other flat ones are closed `unresolved` (`inconclusive`) by `close-rest`; the review corrections were not run on the real tree; the time fit after a dropped class has no test of its own beyond the one drop; Linux only |
| A verifier's site in the same function as a recorded writer, more than three lines away, counted as a site disagreement | corrected | A real run: the finding's site was the declaration of a static cache (line 53), its recorded writers lines 111 and 149, and the verifier named the call that adds the entry, line 116 in the same function; the report printed `Verified: DISAGREES (site ...)` for a finding the verifier agreed with on holder, mechanism and label. `dla ledger verify` now also takes the verifier's site as the finding's own when it lies in the same function (same file, same name, same first line, read from the source) as the finding's site or one of its recorded writers or removers; a site in another function still disagrees. Test `a_verifier_site_in_the_function_of_a_recorded_writer_is_the_same_place` (six lines from the writer in the same function agrees, another function of the same file does not), seen failing with two marker mutations (the function check disabled; any two sites of one file taken as one function), then reversed | Not replayed on the real run; a site outside any function, or a file the parser cannot read, falls back to the three-line rule |
| A JVM holder read `not_loaded` ("the workload never loaded that code") although its class was loaded | corrected | Three causes, all seen in a real run on a Java library, where they sent the queue to ask for hand-written drivers that could never read anything else. (1) A holder in an instance field has a `jvm-class` probe, which reads the static fields of the class: a loaded class without one gives no record and no limitation, and `dla trend` took the missing column for an unloaded class. Now, when no limitation of the series says `class not loaded: <class>`, the verdict is the coverage's (`executed`, `not_executed`, `unknown`) and the detail says the holder is not a static field and its size is not measured. (2) A holder whose owner is a class initialiser has a `jvm-static` probe for a field named `<clinit>`: the agent says `no such field`, which also means the class loaded; same verdict by coverage. (3) The agent looked a probe's class up by the name the probe gives (`pkg.Outer.Inner`) among classes the JVM names `pkg.Outer$Inner`, so every nested class read `class not loaded` and no static holder inside a nested class was ever measured. `findClass` now tries the name with its dots turned into `$` from the right, and the record keeps the probe's spelling, which the column match uses. Tests: `a_jvm_class_probe_with_no_column_and_no_unloaded_limitation_gives_the_coverage_verdict`, `a_jvm_class_probe_whose_class_the_agent_says_is_not_loaded_stays_not_loaded`, `a_jvm_static_probe_of_a_field_the_class_does_not_have_gives_the_coverage_verdict`, and with the real JDK agent `a_class_probe_of_a_loaded_class_without_a_static_field_says_nothing_about_loading` and `a_probe_finds_a_nested_class_and_keeps_the_name_the_probe_gave`; each rule seen failing with a marker mutation, then reversed. Replay of the run's stored series: the two instance-field targets that had read `not_loaded` read `executed`; targets whose class the agent reported unloaded keep `not_loaded` | A class unloaded at the first point and loaded later keeps the series-wide limitation and reads `not_loaded`; the nested-class fix was not replayed on the real library (the stored series were recorded by the old agent); an instance-field holder is still not measured, only no longer misreported; Linux only |
| The trigger and the bundle of a test-loop finding named the test classes the queue chose for it, and the verifier read them as the callers that feed the holder | corrected | A real run on a Java library: the test loop read `grew` for the static cache `TypedPropertyPaths.resolved`, and the trigger line and `test-loop/<H>/tests.json` named the two classes chosen for it by name; run alone, the nine classes show that another class (a Kotlin test chosen for a different finding) adds about 13 entries per run to it and the two named add none, so the verifier read the wrong files and concluded an intended cache. A class chosen for a finding is not a class that feeds it. Now `dla alloc-test <H> --finding <F>` on a test loop reads the holder's reachable object count (the agent's `reachableFromStatic`, the figure its probe record reports as `reachable`, without loading the class) when each test class starts and finishes, sums the difference per class apart for the first run and the later runs, and writes `harness/<H>/attribution-<F>.json`; its output and its discriminator evidence carry `fed_by` (classes with entries per run, largest first) and `ran_flat`; the trigger line names the feeders with their entries per run, or says no single class added any, or says which class feeds it is not measured and prints the command; the bundle holds `fed_by` and `ran_and_added_nothing`, or only `classes_run` with a note that it was not measured; nothing in either names a class by the queue's choice. Tests: `harness_junit` `the_loop_measures_which_test_class_feeds_a_holder_*` (real Gradle project: a class that adds a new key per run, one that repeats a key, one that runs before the holder loads; no file without the ask) and `tests_that_run_in_parallel_*`, `trigger::tests::*test_loop_trigger*` and `a_class_chosen_for_a_finding_*`, `alloc_cmd::tests::*attribut*`, `trigger` `the_bundle_and_the_trigger_of_a_test_loop_finding_name_what_was_measured_*`; each seen failing with a marker mutation (first run against later runs, a negative reading counted as 0, the figure read, the variable name, a positive sum only, the feeder order, three names, the no-feeder text, the not-measured text, the dropped class, the file of a class, the rounding, the bundle note, the ran-flat list, the test-loop condition, the printed bound), then reversed. Real tree (the same library, one harness of the nine classes, `F160`): `alloc-test H10 --finding F160` printed `fed_by` `KPropertyReferenceUnitTests` 14.4 per run and, at first, `TypedPropertyPathUnitTests` 0.1 per run, which is a one-time fill in a later run and not a feed. The loop now also counts the later runs a class added in (`grew_in`); a feeder is a class that added in more than half of them, and a class that added in fewer is listed apart (`added_once` in the output, `added_in_a_few_runs_only` in the bundle; test `a_class_that_added_in_a_few_runs_only_is_a_step_and_not_a_feeder`). On a fresh harness of the same nine classes `fed_by` is the Kotlin class alone (14.5 per run, in 33 of 33 later runs), the Java class is `added_once` (8 entries in 6 runs) and 7 classes are in `ran_flat`; the trigger line reads `the project's own tests KPropertyReferenceUnitTests (14.4 entries per run)`. The verifier agent, given the bundle of a fresh run confirmed by the printed route, labelled the cache `L-COLLECT` in 3 of 3 runs (the key is the caller's object compared by identity, a Kotlin property reference is a new object per call, soft references bound it only by the heap) and kept a control cache of the same library, fed by a test class that builds a new factory per test, `N-INTENT` in 3 of 3 runs; with the earlier bundle (the classes chosen by name) it had read the first cache as `N-INTENT` in 3 of 3; with attribution the command took 23 to 24 s, without it 21 to 22 s (the first one 56 s, with the build). | The verifier check is two findings of one library, three runs each, not a measure of the verifier in general; "more than half of the later runs" is a rule of thumb for telling a feed from a late fill, tested on one real case; the figures are per class run in a loop, so a class that feeds the holder only after another class has run reads as flat; parallel tests overlap and are marked, not separated (the configuration parameter is read from the launcher, an `@Execution` annotation on a class is not seen); only a `jvm-static` probe on a test loop is attributed; the `alloc-test` command with a real test loop is covered by the real tree run and unit tests of its parts, not by a test with a JDK in `alloc_test_jvm.rs`; a negative reading before the holder's class loads is pinned (after-side), the before-side has no test of its own; Linux only |
| A workload blocker recorded for one runtime closed the queue of every runtime | corrected | A real run on a Python library with C extension modules ended after 4 minutes with a complete report and 329 of 346 ranked candidates untested. Two runtime queues existed: python (186 candidates, two harnesses had run and written series) and native (160 candidates in the C sources, which only Python can drive). The agent recorded `build_fails` on `intake.workload` for the native driver; the text said it closed the queue of the runtime, the code closed every queue, and `dla ledger close-rest` then closed 567 findings. The refusal `workload-ran` did not fire because it needed a series for every runtime with candidates. Now the answer names a runtime (`dla ledger resolve intake.workload --status unresolved --reason <blocker> --remedy <text> --runtime <rt>`, stored in the checklist answer): it is required when more than one runtime has ranked candidates (the refusal lists them with their pending counts), implied when one has, refused for an unknown name and for any other item or a finding; `workload-ran` is per runtime and names the runtime, the harness and the series; the queue, the second look and test loop, the trigger line, the gate rules `harness_not_generated`, `harness_not_run`, `budget_left_unrevisited`, `coverage_contradiction`, the report's `Blocked:` line and the build step read the answer for their own runtime only; an answer without a runtime (an older ledger) still applies to every runtime. `close-rest` follows from the queue: it closes the blocked runtime's candidates, leaves the others pending and names the open queue's command. Every text that offers the blocker carries `--runtime <rt>`, and the skill and agent texts say it closes one queue and is refused after a series. Tests: `workload_runtime.rs` (both-runtime refusal, python refused with harness and series while native is accepted, close-rest leaves python, an answer without a runtime closes both, one runtime implied, unknown name and wrong item refused), `trigger_items.rs` (three trigger cases), `gate_harness.rs` (a blocker for another runtime does not lift the harness-not-run rule), pins on the fix texts in `keep_going.rs`, `focus.rs`, `harness_run_attempt.rs` and `skill_text.rs`; each rule seen failing with a marker mutation, then reversed. | Not replayed on the real run (its tree is not here); the tests use a small Python and C project, and the C file is not driven by any harness; a worker's own ledger slice was not exercised with `--runtime`; `--runtime` on an open item is refused by the same list as on a finding but has no test of its own |
| A Python leak that no container holds was reachable only through a second capture run | corrected | A Python library with C extension modules (Python 3.12 in a virtual environment, installed in editable mode, pytest) has a documented leak: every call of one public function leaks one Python object, because a C function of the extension takes a reference and never releases it. Nothing in the program holds the objects, so no census candidate names the line. By hand the route was long: the whole-process column `py.traced` passed, the residual row opened an item, the item asked for a second run with `--capture alloc,histogram` (16 minutes for a unit that runs the project's tests), `dla summarize alloc-stack-diff` listed the line third after two lines of the test runner with `"tied":[]`, and in an unattended run the agent stopped at the open item as inconclusive. Now the driver agent counts the live traced blocks per allocation site at every checkpoint and the run writes `py.blocks@<file:line>` for the sites that grew (mechanism: every checkpoint record carries its own counts, `group_counts`, at most 5000 sites; the agent keeps nothing between checkpoints; `dla harness run` picks the sites that grew over the run, at most 12, and gives each a value at every point). On the library with a unit of one call that draws text with a font (9 points of 440 units, key mode fresh): `py.blocks@<file>:580` passes with a slope of 1.02 blocks per unit, and `dla trend` prints `dla ledger add --origin growth-series --evidence E --site <file>:580 --region heap --holder <kind>` with the reading rule for the holder; the final gate lists the same line with the same command. With a unit that runs two of the project's test files in the same process (about 3.3 s per unit, 9 points of 2 units): the same column passes with a slope of 126 blocks per unit (about 145 was expected beforehand; this is what was measured), the test runner's own lines have no column (they lie outside the project), and 2 sites grew, so the bound of 12 and its limitation line were not reached on the real tree. The route by hand ran to `ledger confirm` without a refusal: `ledger add` with the holder `native-handle/global-ref` (the line calls a function of an extension module and nothing holds the result), the non-leak sibling, `ledger test`, `dla alloc-test H1 --finding F` (7 s, `decided_by` the site column, favors leak), refute of the sibling, `ledger confirm F --evidence E` with the trend's evidence. `ledger label F --as L-OWNERSHIP` is refused (`label-runtime-row`): the python reference has a row for L-COLLECT only; the route stops there. Cost (milliseconds per unit, mean of 3 runs for the first unit, 1 run for the second, snapshot and grouping at every checkpoint included): 1 frame 0.66 and 3313, 2 frames 0.94 (1.42x) and 4417 (1.33x), 4 frames 1.26 and 6133, 8 frames 2.38 and 10222; the snapshot and the grouping took 78 ms and 680 ms per checkpoint at 1 frame. Neither unit stays within 1.25 times the time at one frame at two frames, so the default depth of a driver run stays 1. A first version kept the counts of every checkpoint in the agent and wrote the series at the last checkpoint; that bookkeeping read as growth (py.traced 91 B per unit instead of 32 to 36 on the leak unit; a unit with no leak passed py.traced, 55 B per unit). Now `py.traced` on the leak unit is 34 to 35 B per unit, and on the two-test-file unit 4054 B per unit (it was 42979). On a no-leak unit (copy, resize, tobytes) the gate passes some OS column in about half of the runs, with the earlier agent as well as with this one (5 of 10 each, interleaved): that noise is in the unit, not in the agent. Order inside a checkpoint: collections, then the snapshot and the grouping (collector off meanwhile), then `py.traced`, `py.traced_peak` and the OS values, so the snapshot's transient memory is in every reading, the first measured one included. A record cut at the 5000-site bound lists the counter in `group_capped`; a site missing there is left empty, not 0. A cut run of the second unit (timeout 40 s) kept 5 of 9 points and 5 site columns. Tests: `harness_python_blocks.rs` (real Python driver: the keeping line has a column with a value at every point and a rise of 50 per round, the freeing line, the test file and a harness file in the project have none, a site that stopped growing at the end of the warm-up has none, the 12-site bound with the largest first and its limitation line, a run with 320 live sites and no leak whose `py.traced` rose by at most 8192 bytes from the second measured point to the last (1901 measured; 141810 when the agent keeps one dict per checkpoint), the depth rule, the stopped-tracing limitation, the editable-install source tree), `py_site_columns.rs` (the add command, the tie by the line only, the final-gate item, the refusal to resolve, `close-rest`, the alloc-test deciding by the site column, `confirm`), unit tests of the series builder (selection: the 12-site cap and its line, strict growth, the first point after the warm-up, zero-filled points, the bound, a cut run), of the candidate rule and of the column choice of `alloc-test`. Each rule was seen failing with a marker mutation: the test-file rule, the harness-file rule, the 12-site cap, the limitation line, strict growth, the first point after the warm-up, points filled with zero or left empty at the bound, a cut run, one dict per checkpoint kept in the agent, the default depth, the not-tracing limitation, the editable source tree, the `.dla` exclusion, the depth limitation line, the cap rank of the site columns, the match of a point to its `x`, the column meaning, the add command in `next`, the order of the columns, the final-gate tie by the line, the final-gate fix, the refusal to resolve, the pick of the site column by `alloc-test`, a site column missing from the new series, the tie by site of `confirm`, the per-site candidate rule | A driver run only (command mode and the other runtimes are unchanged). With one frame a block that a library function allocates for project code has no site (a limitation line says so; `DLA_TRACEMALLOC_FRAMES=<n>` keeps more, at the costs above; the depth rule is tested with 2 frames on a copy of a list made by the standard library). A library installed in editable mode runs from its source tree, not from the scratch copy: the harness run now passes that tree as `DLA_SOURCE_ROOT`, and the agent counts its files (the run directory `.dla`, `.git`, installed packages, test files and the harness are never counted); without it the real tree gave no column. A test file follows the Python rule of `dla workload discover` (`FileInfo.is_test`: a directory named `test`, `tests`, `__tests__`, `__mocks__`, `testing`, `spec` or `specs`, `test_*`, `*_test`, `conftest.py`, `setup.py`, `__main__.py`), which is case sensitive: a directory `Tests` is not one. An `alloc-test` whose new series has no column for the finding's line (the agent writes only the sites that grew) has nothing to vote with and reads inconclusive; this is tested on the column choice only, not through a run. The Python tests are Unix only |
| Plan deviation: the archetype L-OWNERSHIP listed only the region `off-heap` | decided | The plan for the allocation-site columns kept the table as it was. A managed object kept alive by a reference that native code never released (a CPython reference count, a JNI global reference) lies in the managed heap, and a finding like the one above has the region `heap` and the holder `native-handle/global-ref`. With the table as it was, labelling it `L-OWNERSHIP` records `region_mismatch` (a warning in the label result and the line `region does not match the archetype's` in the report) for a label that is right. `data/archetypes.toml` now lists `off-heap` and `heap` for L-OWNERSHIP. Test: `l_ownership_fits_a_managed_object_kept_alive_by_native_code_as_well_as_native_memory` (failed with `heap` removed from the row). The generated block of `agents/verifier.md` lists only the summaries and did not change | The holders, the required evidence and the summary of the row are as before; a native allocation without an owner stays `off-heap`. The python reference still has no L-OWNERSHIP row, so the label itself needs `--note` or UNCLASSIFIED there |
| The Python queue had no first pass over the project's own tests, and a leak that nothing holds was never reached | corrected | A real unattended run on a Python library with C extension modules (editable install, Python 3.12, pytest) wrote its own driver units, never called the function that leaks (a C function takes a reference and never releases it), and found nothing; run by hand in one process, two of the library's test files show the leak as the top growing project line (about 126 to 145 blocks per round). Added: `dla harness gen --lang python --pytest [--tests a,b]` (hookless; one `pytest.main` of the test files of the working directory with the most of them, in a loop; manifest tool `pytest-loop`; step 1, timeout 1800 s), the driver `harness/python/dla_pytest_loop.py` (drops a file none of whose tests passed, drops the slowest files to fit the time limit, stops with a `DLA-PYTEST` line and exit 2 or 3 when no test passed, none was found, the tests import the project from outside it or one file alone does not fit), the first command of an open Python queue that has test files (`dla run status`, the gate item, `close-rest`; it stays until a pytest-loop harness wrote a series or an attempt failed, including a refused generation, which is recorded in `harness/pytest-loop-attempt.json`), the scratch copy of a Python harness keeping built extension modules (untracked `*.so`, `*.pyd`, `*.dylib` next to Python sources; the clone of `dla intervene` too), the loop's file in the bundle (`test-loop/<H>/tests.json` with `test_files_run`), the trigger text (no `dla alloc-test` hint), and three small items: the commands for a finding added from a line that grew in the loop name a small driver of its own first (`--probe F` was checked to accept a finding with a site and no container, and its hooks comment names the line), the file-name rule of the agent's test-file test is pinned (`test_*`, `*_test`, `conftest.py` in a directory named `Tests`), and `producer_hint` prints `dla trend <S>` (the command with `--finding` is refused without `--against`). Verified: `harness_pytest_loop.rs` (real loop on a small project: no edit, the keeping line rises one block per run at every point, the failing file is dropped with its limitation, every test failing and the outside copy fail with the `DLA-PYTEST` line, a slow file is dropped to fit a limit, one file that alone does not fit gives no point, a later failing run is counted once, a step of 0 runs one unit and a step too long for the limit is lowered, generation refusals, the copy), `pytest_loop.rs` (binary: the queue, the refusal with the install text, the finding, the small driver, `--probe`), unit tests in `queue.rs`, `trigger_tests.rs`, `grown.rs`, `labels.rs`, `run.rs`, `keep_going.rs`, `trigger.rs`, `harness_python_blocks.rs`; each rule seen failing with a marker mutation, then reversed. The queue reads the test files from the workload discovery result, and nothing wrote that result in a run that never called `dla workload discover` (the agents of earlier real runs did not call it), so `dla census --lang python` now writes it when the run has none and the project has Python test files, whatever the census counted (the sweep alone can raise the candidates the queue works on); a project without test files gets no discovery result from the census, as before. On the real library, in a fresh run with `dla probe`, `dla sweep` and `dla census` only: the queue named the loop first; the loop ran the whole test suite (158 test paths, 149 files with results, 3736 tests per run) 9 times in 21 minutes with no edit and no file dropped (a run of the tests outside the harness takes 32 seconds; in the loop 105 s at the start and 170 s at the end, as the heap grows); 12 `py.blocks@` columns passed and the line of the leaking call had the second largest slope (299 blocks per run); a finding added at that line with the printed command, a small driver made with `--probe F` and a unit that makes that call gave 1.03 blocks per unit, `dla alloc-test` decided by the site column for a leak in 7 seconds, and the finding reached `confirmed`; after the loop the queue command was the driver batch. Most of the other growing lines had one cause in the library itself: a module-level object stores an import error and raises it again on every use, each raise adds the caller's stack to the stored error, and those frames keep every pytest session alive (about 120000 objects and 80 MB per run of the suite, measured without the agent too). `dla trend` and the final gate had listed the site columns in name order, which put the leak line tenth of twelve and outside the three printed add commands; they now list them largest growth first, a line that already has a finding before the others. Also: with a `python3` that has no pytest the command is refused and the queue goes on with the driver batches. `dla-bench dynamic --only python`: 9 of 9 pass. | A second unattended run on that library, with everything of this row except the last four items, found the documented leak without the loop: the Python worker ran `dla ledger list` and a stage gate after the census, not `dla run status`, and took the driver batch, because the worker instructions named that command literally and nothing it read named the loop command; its own driver units made the leaking call, the site column of the calling line passed (3.05 blocks per unit), the worker added the finding with the printed command, confirmed it, reproduced it through the public API, and named the C function that takes a reference and never releases it; the run took 18 minutes. Added after that run: the worker instructions say to run the command `dla run status` prints for the queue, the census note and `dla ledger list` (`totals.queue.<runtime>.command`) name the queue's command, and a Python hook driver generated while the loop is still the first step carries `pytest_hint` (never cut by the output bound). The gate items of that run also named the driver batch while the loop was pending, and the worker followed them: now, while the loop is the queue's first step, the fix of a Python finding that waits for a harness to be generated is the queue item's fix, the loop first and then the driver batch (`gate_items_of_python_findings_name_the_loop_first_while_it_is_pending`), and a harness that is already generated keeps its own run command (`next_declares_the_targets_prints_them_and_writes_the_block_in_the_driver`). Not seen yet: an agent running the loop by itself. Only Python 3.12 and pytest 9 on Linux were run; `*.pyd` and `*.dylib` are copied by the same rule and untested; a project whose tests need a running service, use pytest-xdist or call `os._exit`, and a unittest-only project were not run. The per-run time of the whole suite grows with the number of live traced blocks, so a suite twice as slow may lose files to the time limit; a drop was only seen on small projects. The `Tests/` helper line of the library's test suite is counted as a project line by the file-name rule (it is no test directory by the rule), and 21 more allocation sites that grew have no column (12 at most). The finding made from the line was decided by the unit tests and the binary tests, not by an alloc-test run on the real library |
| The automatic budget of a run with workers stayed at one hour | corrected | A real unattended run on a project with two runtimes (python and native) dispatched two workers; the python worker had 186 ranked candidates, so the formula gives the cap of 180 minutes, yet the run ended with `budget.seconds` 3600, `settled` true, `estimate` 0 runtimes and 0 candidates, 25 minutes of share per worker, and the report said "175 findings were closed for lack of time". Two causes: a worker sized the budget only when its gate check passed, which does not happen while its queue is open (its gate lists the candidates that have no disposition), and the core sized it from its own ledger as soon as sweep and census had run, where a dispatched run has no candidate, and made that final, so no worker could raise it. Now in a run with registered workers the core never sizes the budget (not the command hook, not the `ready` rule of its gate check, not a passing gate check at `sweep` or later), and each worker sizes it with its first command after the sweep and census of its own runtime, whether a gate check passes or not and whatever the command is (`dla ledger list`, `dla run status`, `dla harness gen`, a gate check), from the main ledger and every worker's slice, raise-only, once per worker, final once every registered worker has; the delta moves the run deadline and every unfinished worker's deadline as before. A run without workers behaves as before (pinned by `the_first_passing_gate_at_sweep_settles_an_auto_budget_once` and `each_command_that_needs_the_budget_settles_it_once_after_sweep_and_census`). Tests: `a_workers_first_command_after_sweep_and_census_sizes_the_run` (a command that is not a gate check; 20 candidates and 2 workers give 110 minutes, both deadlines move by the same 50 minutes, `settled` stays false until the second worker, a repeat changes nothing, the second worker raises it), `a_failing_gate_check_of_the_worker_sizes_the_run_too`, `the_core_of_a_dispatched_run_never_sizes_the_budget`; each failed with the change it covers undone. `settling_moves_the_deadline_of_every_unfinished_worker` now sizes through a worker's gate check, because it had pinned the core sizing a dispatched run | A worker that never runs a command after its sweep and census never sizes it; the real run was not repeated |
| A Python report said that no entry point was exercised when the package was installed in editable mode | corrected | The report of the same real run opened with "exercised 0 of 739 entry points (python)" and "none called an entry point", which was false: the drivers called the public API in every unit. The harness runs in a scratch copy, the package was imported from the source tree, and the coverage callback of the Python agent recorded only functions of files under the copy. The agent now records functions of files under the source root too (`DLA_SOURCE_ROOT`, the rule the allocation-site columns already use), by their path relative to it. Test: `coverage_lists_functions_of_the_source_root_outside_the_scratch_copy` (failed with the source-root case removed and with the `.dla` exclusion removed). On the real library a hook driver that makes the leaking call now marks 8 entry points as exercised, where the run had none | Not covered by a test of `dla harness run` end to end; a Python before 3.12 has no function coverage at all |
| A Python registration table grown by the driver's own keys was reported as reproduced through the public API | corrected | A real unattended run on a Python library confirmed ten module-level tables (an extension table among them) with the line "reproduced through the public API": each is a dict at module level with one public registration function and a plural wrapper that calls it, plugin modules call the wrapper at import time, and the driver called the wrapper with a new key per unit. The table grows by design when a caller registers a new key, and the project itself registers constants at start-up. The tool already reads that case as `grown only by calling the registration function itself`, which needs the census fact `startup` on the writer site and a driver that calls the writer. Two things were missing. First, the trigger matched the driver's calls against the writer function only, so a call of the wrapper was no call of the writer; the census now records the wrappers between the writer and its start-up callers in the new site field `startup_via`, and the trigger reads a call of one of them as a call of the writer. Second, in the real run six of the seven registration writers of the library had `startup`; the seventh had none because a function of one plugin module that is registered as the module's save handler calls the registration function at run time with a key taken from its argument, which is a real run-time caller and a correct reading (calls in test files never counted: the census reads no caller there, by its test-file rule, and a test pins it). A smaller correction on the way: the census counted callers by bare name only, so a same-named function of an unrelated module called at run time made a writer not start-up; it now drops a call of the name that provably reaches another module (a module alias, an import of the name from elsewhere, a function the calling file defines itself). The same real run showed the tables with `startup` grown by a batch hook driver whose unit called the registration function itself, confirmed with "Trigger: not checked (no entry-path harness was run for it)": the trigger now reads that unit of any hook driver (not a test loop) as `direct` too, for every finding that grew in a series of that driver, whether the driver declared it as a target or a passing column of its trend is only tied to it (on the real library one registration call fills two tables, and the second one, not a target of the driver, was still confirmable until the tie counted), and an entry-path harness that grew the finding without the call, or the project's own workload, still wins as `reproduced`. Tests: five census tests over a small package and one for test files (`python_a_writer_called_only_by_a_wrapper_that_modules_call_at_the_top_level_is_start_up`, `python_a_call_in_a_test_file_is_no_run_time_caller` and four more), the trigger tests of the wrapper and of the hook driver (`a_unit_that_calls_the_wrapper_of_a_start_up_writer_calls_the_registration_function_itself`, `a_hook_driver_whose_unit_calls_a_start_up_writer_is_the_direct_reading`, `an_entry_path_harness_that_grew_it_without_the_call_wins_over_a_direct_hook_driver`); each failed with the change it covers undone, except the pins of what was already so (a run-time caller stays reproduced; a test loop is never direct, because the project's own workload returns first). The same run lost nine minutes because the Python test loop was started under the shell's `timeout 560`; the loop runs up to 1800 s, and the two reference texts now say never to do that (pinned in `skill_text`) | The real library was not run again after the hook-driver rule; calls through an object, an aliased `from m import f as g`, and a wrapper called only through an object method are read as before; `startup_via` is recorded for Python only |
| A finding grown only by a unit that calls its own start-up registration function could be confirmed as a leak | decided | `dla ledger confirm F` is refused with `registration-only` when the trigger of F is `direct`: the census reads every writer of the holder as a start-up registration and the only growth measured comes from a unit that calls that function itself with new keys, which is growth by design. The fix gives one route, because no tool command yields a discriminator that favors non-leak for a table driven with new keys (the alloc-test of the direct driver and the keys comparison favor leak): an entry-path harness whose unit uses the public API without calling the registration function, `dla harness run <H> --mode both`, `dla trend <S>`, `dla alloc-test <H> --finding F`. If the table grows there, `dla ledger confirm F` is allowed; if it stays flat, that alloc-test favors non-leak, `dla ledger refute F --evidence <it>` succeeds, and an `N-INTENT` sibling (the id the add prints, not a fixed one) is tested and confirmed with the same evidence, which is how the ledger already closes a leak whose non-leak sibling is confirmed. The `next` list of `dla alloc-test` prints the entry-path command and the alloc-test that follows it instead of the confirm. The rule follows the census facts, so it applies to every runtime that has them. Tests: `a_registry_grown_only_by_its_own_writer_is_not_confirmed_and_the_non_leak_way_closes_it` (runs the route with real commands, an earlier warm-up sibling present, and ends with the leak finding refuted and the sibling `.n2` confirmed; an earlier version of this test appended a hand-made discriminator, which no tool produces) and `a_registry_an_entry_path_harness_grew_without_the_direct_call_is_confirmed_as_before`; each failed with the refusal, the direct reading of the refusal, or the alloc-test branch undone | `dla trend` printed no confirm command, so it is unchanged; a finding grown by a hook driver whose census facts are missing is not refused (the trigger is `unchecked`) |
| A test loop narrowed to a few files counted as the queue's first step | corrected | A real unattended run on a Python project with test files generated the loop over all of them (158 test paths), did not run it, generated a second loop narrowed with `--tests` to three files, ran that one and went on to the driver batches, because the queue ended its first step when any pytest loop wrote a series. The three files did not reach the leaking call, so the run never saw its line, which an earlier run with the full loop did. The first step now ends only when the loop over all test files wrote a series or an attempt of that loop failed; a narrowed loop that ran or failed changes nothing, and `dla harness run <H>` or `dla harness gen --lang python --pytest` stays the printed command while the full loop is pending. A narrowed loop is still allowed, and its summary carries the kept-whole hint (`pytest_hint`) while the full loop is the first step. Manifest: a narrowed loop lists the tool `pytest-narrowed` next to `pytest-loop`. Tests: the `queue` unit tests of the narrowed loop and `a_narrowed_loop_does_not_replace_the_loop_over_all_test_files`. |
| A test loop cut by the shell was recorded as a blocker and closed the queue | corrected | A real unattended run on a Python library: the first step of the queue is the project's own tests in a loop (`dla harness gen --lang python --pytest`, then `dla harness run <H>`), and one run of the suite takes about two minutes inside the loop, the whole series about twenty. The worker generated the loop and started it with the shell's `&` in one tool call, because two reference texts told it to "start it in the background". The shell job died when the call returned: the harness directory held ticks and no `attempt.json`. The worker then tried to close a finding `time_budget` (refused with `harness-not-run`) and took the other way that refusal offered, a blocker `env_unavailable` on `intake.workload` for python, which was accepted, closed the python queue, and ended the run in 28 minutes with nothing measured and more than two hours of the deadline left. Two causes. The texts: python.md, stages.md and the worker agent now say to run the loop as a plain foreground command with the tool's longest timeout, to wait for the notice when the tool moves it to the background, and never to use `&`, `nohup` or `timeout`; a pin in `skill_text` also checks that the words "in the background and wait" are gone. The ledger: a test loop needs nothing written, so "no harness can be made" cannot hold for it, and a run that never started or was cut shows nothing about the environment. `dla ledger resolve intake.workload --status unresolved --reason <a blocker reason>` is now refused with `loop-not-finished` while the runtime of the answer (every runtime when it names none) has a generated test loop (`HarnessManifest::is_test_loop`) with neither a series nor a recorded finished attempt (no `attempt.json`, or one that says `interrupted`, `hooks_unfilled` or `time_budget`); the fix is `dla harness run <H>` as a plain foreground command, and the blocker is accepted once the loop has a recorded failure, as before. `harness-not-run` for a test loop no longer offers the blocker (its missing and fix text name the run only); for a hook driver both are unchanged. Tests: `a_blocker_is_refused_while_a_generated_test_loop_has_no_end_and_accepted_after_a_failed_attempt` (both loop kinds, every blocker reason, an interrupted record), `a_blocker_is_accepted_for_a_runtime_with_only_a_hook_driver_that_never_ran`, `harness_not_run_for_a_test_loop_offers_no_blocker_and_for_a_hook_driver_it_still_does` (`gate_harness`), `a_blocker_for_python_is_refused_while_its_test_loop_has_no_end_and_one_for_native_is_accepted` (`workload_runtime`). |
| Attribute names built at run time read as growing lines | corrected | A real unattended run on a Python library ended with 189 open items, 175 of them `py.blocks@<file:line>` columns of four lines that each build an attribute name at run time and look it up, repeated over about fifty trends. The interpreter keeps a reference to the name string of an attribute lookup in its type attribute cache (4096 slots, the slot chosen by the address of the string), and a name built at run time is a new string object each time, so the cache keeps up to 4096 of them alive: at a checkpoint the line showed a few dozen to a few hundred live blocks, a number that wandered and crept up and that `dla trend` read as `stair`, `plateau` or `steady`. A ten-line script showed the blocks are held by the cache and by nothing else: 15, 30, 169 and 206 after four rounds of 2000 calls, 0 right after the cache was cleared, 663 after the next round. The Python agent now empties the interpreter's caches right after the two forced collections of a checkpoint and before any figure is read (`sys._clear_internal_caches()` where the interpreter has it, else `sys._clear_type_cache()`, never the deprecated one on an interpreter that has the new one; it never raises, and an interpreter with neither gets one limitation line per process). Tick records are unchanged; the pytest loop calls the same `checkpoint`. Tests: `a_line_that_builds_an_attribute_name_at_run_time_and_keeps_nothing_has_no_column` (`harness_python_blocks`, together with the keeping line, whose column and values the first test of that file pins). |
| A growing writer line of a known holder asked for a second finding | corrected | In the same run (and in two earlier ones, with 2, 10 and 24 such items) a unit registered a new key per call in a table, so the line that writes into the table grew and its `py.blocks@<file:line>` column was listed as tied to no finding, with the command that adds a finding at that line, although the census finding of the table existed. The column ties to a finding only when the finding's own site is the line, and the site of the table finding is the line that defines the table. The new finding had no census facts, so the rules that depend on them (`registration-only`, for one) did not apply to it. One predicate, `grown::finding_is_at_site`, now says a leak-side finding is at a site when its own site is that line or the census annotation of the finding lists the line among the writers of its holder (`writer_sites` or the plain `writers`; removers never count; a sibling hypothesis never ties). The final gate (`tied_findings`), the grown-unmeasured rule (a finding closed `not_exercised` cannot hide a line that grows), the trigger rule and the candidates `dla trend` lists for a site column use it, so the gate asks for no new finding at such a line, the column waits on the existing finding while it has no disposition, and `dla trend` leads with that finding instead of printing the add command. The alloc-test pick finds these columns through the candidates of the trend. A finding at another line that is not a writer line still does not tie the column. Tests: `a_finding_is_at_its_own_site`, `a_finding_is_at_a_writer_line_of_its_holder_listed_plainly_or_in_the_census_facts`, `a_sibling_is_never_at_a_site`, `a_site_that_is_neither_has_no_finding_and_a_remover_does_not_count` (`grown`), `a_column_at_a_writer_line_of_a_finding_is_tied_to_it_and_the_gate_asks_for_no_second_finding`, `a_remover_line_or_a_finding_without_that_writer_does_not_tie_the_column` (`py_site_columns`). |
